## Document 019: 2026-10-01_［みずほ経済EXPRESS］対日直接投資拡大で国内供給力の強化へ_f76b5ad3

みずほ経済 EXPRESS

対日直接投資拡大で国内供給力の強化へ 
～戦略 17 分野でみた外資誘致の現状と課題～

調査部  エコノミスト 阿部大樹 
080-3709-6077 taiki.abe@mizuho-rt.co.jp 
■ 高まる国内投資の機運。 国内供給力強化に向けては対内直接投資の取り込みも重要に 
政府は2026年7月、 日本成長戦略会議において、 国内供給力の強化に向けた官民投資ロードマップを
決定した。とりわけ経済安全保障上の重要性や成長期待が高い17の戦略分野においては、2040年度ま
でに累計370兆円超の官民投資が想定されている（図表1）。日本政策投資銀行「2026年度設備投資計
画調査」では、大企業の約58％、中堅企業の約43％が戦略分野における国内投資を強化すると答えて
おり、多くの企業が政府の方針を前向きに受け止めていることがうかがえる。 
国内投資促進の主軸を担うのは政府や国内民間企業だが、国内供給力を強化していくうえでは、外
国資本の取り込み、すなわち対内直接投資の拡大を促すことも重要だ。対内直接投資とは、外国企業
が日本企業への出資や融資を通じて、日本国内の企業経営・事業に継続的に関わることを目的とした
投資のことをいう。政府も対日直接投資推進会議で、戦略分野ごとに有望な外国企業を発掘し誘致す
る方針を掲げている。 
対内直接投資は、 投資形態の違いから四つに分類できる （図表2） 。 このうち、 「合併・買収」 や 「企

### Page 2

2

業・財務再編」は国内供給力の強化につながらない可能性もあるが、「グリーンフィールド投資」と
「能力増強投資」は工場・生産ラインなど生産資本の増加をともない、供給網のレジリエンス向上や
国内の供給力強化につながりやすい形態だ。本稿では、これら二つの形態を合わせて「国内供給力強
化投資」と呼ぶこととする。以下では「国内供給力強化投資」に焦点を絞り、戦略分野ごとに直近5年
間の投資流入状況を確認したうえで、対内直接投資の今後の増加余地と誘致のあり方を検討する。 
■ AI・データセンター関連投資等のグローバルな機運が国内供給力強化投資をけん引  
日本の対内直接投資は、2010年代半ば以降、拡大傾向にある。国際収支統計で集計される対内直接
投資の動向を示した図表3をみると、 その力強い伸びが顕著に見て取れる。GDP比でも1.0％ 程 度（2025
年）まで増加しており、外国企業の国内投資志向が高まりつつある証左と言えよう。 
先行きは、さらなる対内直接投資の拡大が期待できそうだ。予定段階の個別投資案件を集計してい
るfDi Marketsのデータで国内供給力強化投資の動向を確認すると、2020年代に入って投資金額が大幅
に拡大していることがわかる （図表4）。同データでみられる投資拡大の動きは、クロスボーダー金融
取引の実績を計上している国際収支統計でも今後顕在化してくるとみられる。 
さらに、個別投資案件のデータを確認することで、国際収支統計では把握できなかった対内直接投
資の性質・内容を詳細に分析することが可能である。戦略分野別に国内供給力強化投資をみると、AI・
半導体分野や、データセンター関連が含まれるデジタル・サイバーセキュリティ分野で投資金額が大
きく、 かつ大幅な伸びが確認できる （図表5） 。 世界的なAIブームを受けたデータセンター関連投資の
活発化が背景とみられる。半導体製造設備やデータセンター関連の投資には巨額の資本が必要になる
ため、投資金額を件数で割った一件あたりの投資額が大規模化しやすく、大型投資案件が投資額全体
を押し上げていることがうかがえる。 
次に投資金額が大きかったのは資源・エネルギー安全保障・GX分野だ。具体的には、発電所などの
電力供給設備への投資案件が押し上げている。太陽光発電・洋上風力発電などの再生可能エネルギー
市場で海外企業の参入が増えた結果とみられ、脱炭素化を志向するグローバルな機運が追い風になっ
図表3 対内直接投資 図表4 国内供給力強化投資

ていると言える。加えて、電力需要の大きいデータセンターの立地を最適化するため、電力源と通信
インフラを一体的に整備する「ワットビット連携」が推進される中で、電力供給設備への投資需要が
高まっていることも当該分野の投資押上げに寄与しているとみられる。 
これらの分野では引き続きグローバルトレンドに沿った投資流入増が見込めそうである一方、造船
分野や防衛産業分野、量子分野、海洋分野の投資額は、増加しているものの依然として小規模だ。例
えば、量子分野は研究開発～実証段階の技術が中心の新領域であり、分野の将来的な成長期待は大き
い。現時点では収益化まで距離があることから、投資額の小ささ自体が問題とは言えないものの、米
国等と比べると日本の産業基盤は相対的に小さく、 スタートアップ企業の集積でも遅れを取っている。
さらに投資を呼び込むうえでは、こうした遅れを埋める手立てを講じることが必要不可欠であろう。 
また、造船分野では、2020年代に入ってから中国の存在感が一段と高まっている。日本の造船会社
による船舶の新規受注量は2000年代半ばから減少しており、世界の船舶建造量に占める日本のシェア
も低下傾向だ。背景には、中国と比べた事業所規模の小ささが指摘されており、生産コストなどの面
で水をあけられている。 
■ 幅広い分野での外国資本取り込みに向けて供給面 ・ 制度面のボトルネック軽減が重要 
戦略分野全体でみれば投資流入の堅調さが確認できた一方で、17の戦略分野それぞれにおいて投資
流入増を実現していく1ためには、 投資の門戸を開くだけでは不十分だ。 造船分野や量子分野に関して
指摘したような課題の解決に向けては、ボトルネックを軽減する地道な取り組みが必要であろう。ま
た、足元で急速に拡大しているデータセンター投資や再エネ関連投資についても、今後は成長の維持
が課題となる。 
外国資本取り込みにおける具体的なボトルネックとして、人材不足等の供給制約や複雑な制度、言
語障壁が挙げられる。日本国内の外資系企業を対象にした、日本貿易振興機構による「2025年度外資
系企業ビジネス実態調査」 では、 直近1～2年の日本のビジネス環境の課題について、 多くの企業が 「高
度人材等の確保」 と 「一般人材の確保」 を回答しているほか、 直近では 「オフィス・事業用地の確保」
図表5 戦略17分野別にみた国内供給力強化投資額の推移

ては除外。各社の個別投資プロジェクトの情報及び、日本成長戦略会議資料を基に、みずほ総合研究所調査部が分類

### Page 4

4

を回答する企業の割合も高まっている。こうした人材・立地面等の供給制約は、外資の取り込みを進
めるうえで課題となる。 
また、制度面について、日本貿易振興機構による「ジェトロ対日投資報告2018」では、外国企業が
日本でビジネス展開するうえでの阻害要因として「行政手続きの複雑さ」や「許認可制度の厳しさ」
等が挙げられ、外国企業にとって日本のビジネス環境への適応が容易ではないことがうかがえる。な
お、 同調査では 「外国語によるコミュニケーションの難しさ」 が2位にランクするなど、 言語障壁も外
国企業の参入を阻む一因だ。 
供給面・制度面のボトルネック解消に向けた対応として、人材不足については、大学等の研究・教
育機関との連携強化を通じた国内人材の育成・確保や、高度外国人材の受入強化による対応が考えら
れる。また、用地不足に対しては、産業用地の確保や空き家・空き地の有効活用を通じて、外国企業
が拠点を設立しやすい環境を整備することが重要だろう。 
制度面の対応としては、まず、手続きの一本化が挙げられる。現状、外国企業等の投資申請に際し
ては担当省庁・公的機関と個別にやり取りするケースがあり、外国企業の手続き負担は小さいとは言
えない。 この点、OECD（2026） は外国企業 ・ 投資家と関係省庁等との調整を担う 「ワンストップ窓口」
の機能強化を提言している。 言語障壁については近年通訳や英訳による手続き支援が進みつつあるが、
主要な政策等については英語アナウンスを適時提供するなど、言語障壁軽減に向けたさらなる取り組
みが求められる。

［参考文献］ 
OECD (2022), FDI Qualities Policy Toolkit, OECD Publishing 
OECD (2025), OECD Benchmark Definition of Foreign Direct Investment,  5th edition, OECD 
Publishing 
OECD (2026), OECD Economic Surveys: Japan 2026, OECD Publishing

### Page 5

5

1 コンテンツ産業のような日本企業に競争力がある分野では、外国資本の取り込みによる事業拡大も期待される一方、独自技術
や知的財産の流出リスクには留意し、慎重に議論を進める必要がある。

주가 추이

견조한 판매 확대로 매출 견조, 이익은 시장 기대치 하회 예상 
3Q26 동사 매출액과 영업이익은 각각 33.6조원(+17.3% 이하 Y oY)과 2.39조
원(+63.6%) 기록 전망. 3Q 매출액은 친환경차 중심 신차 확대 통해 두 자릿수 
성장세를 기록. 3Q 판매량은 도매 기준 84.3만대(+7.6% 이하 Y oY)로 전년 동
기 대비 성장 폭을 확대했고, 특히 친환경차 판매량 29.8만대(+45.9%)를 기록
하며 이번 분기 매출 성장에 절대적으로 기여.  
다만, 3Q 수익성은 4~5월 상승했던 원자재 투입 영향과 ‘경제형EV 믹스’가 더
욱 확대(13.2% → 14.5%)되면서 단기 수익성에 부정적 작용하며 시장 기대치 
2.5조원을 다소 하회할 전망. 그러나, 기말 환율이 1,356원/$ 수준으로 낮 아 지
면서 전년 동기 수 익 성 악화에 크게 영 향 을 미쳤던 판매보증비 외화환산손익 
영향이 개선(1.6%p 수준) 기대되며, 관세 부담 또한 북미 HEV 확대로 전년 동
기 대비 약 4,500억원(2.0%p) 감소하면서 부정적 수익성 영향 만회 전망.  
유럽은 점유율 확대, 북미는 수익성 개선으로 
올해 북미 HEV와 유럽 BEV 중심 신차 출시 및 물량 확대가 본격화되면
서, ’27년에는 이익 성장 기여도가 확대될 것으로 전망. 미국 조 지 아 텔 루 라 이
드 HEV 신규 생산(2.8만대) 및 HMGMA 스 포 티 지HEV신규 생산(1.0만
대) 등 수 요 가 높은 북미 현 지 에 서 HEV 물량 확대 중으로 4Q에도 텔루라이
드 HEV와 스포티지 HEV의 현지 생산/판매 확대를 비롯하여 셀토스 HEV 
출시로 ’27년 북미 중심 HEV 신차 물량 증가 및 믹스 개선 기대. 유럽은 강한 
BEV 수요가 지속되면서 중국 업체들의 공세 강화 중이나 동사의 물량 확대 또
한 견조. 3Q EV3 2.9만대, EV5 2.2만대 등 유럽 BEV 수요 대응 전략 주효. ’27
년에는 K4/셀토스 및 쏘렌토 등 HEV 신차 중 심 의 친환경차 PT 믹스 다양화
로 유럽 내 물량 확대 흐름 지속 전망 
물량 확대는 증익으로 귀결, 기말 배당 매력도 부각 
’27년 동사 영업이익 추 정 치 는 11.1조원(+13.5%)으로 증익 지속 전망. 최근 
환율 추세 고려 시 내년 수익성에 부 정 적 영향 확대 가능성 존재하나, 올해 확
인된 신차 중심 물량 확대를 통해 부정적 환율 영향을 상쇄할 것 으 로 기대. 
12M EPS 추정치 하향(-2.9%) 및 상 반 기 적 용 한 코스피 프리미엄 제거(-
10%)로 목 표 주 가 를 260,000원으로 하향. 그러나, RA 설립에서의 동사 지분 
참여 및 기말 배당(’26년 DPS 7,000원 추정)에 따른 상 대 적 배당 수익 매력
도 부각 등은 주가 모 멘 텀 에 있어 기대 가능한 요소.

[ 종목 투자등급 ] 
당사는 개별 종목에 대해 향후 1 년간 +15% 이상의 절대수익률이 기대되는 종목에 대해 Buy(매수) 의견을 제시합니다. 또한 절대수
익률 -15~+15%가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Hold(보유) 의견을, -15% 이하가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Sell(매도) 의견을 제시합니다. 밸류
에이션 방법 등 절대수익률 산정은 개별 종목을 커버하는 애널리스트의 추정에 따르며, 목표주가 산정이나 투자의견 변경 주기는 종
목별로 다릅니다.

[ 산업 투자의견 ] 
당사는 산업에 대해 향후 1 년간 해당 업종의 수익률이 과거 수익률에 비해 양호한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Positive(긍정
적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 또한 향후 1 년간 수익률이 과거 수익률과 유사한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Neutral(중립적) 
의견을, 과거 수익률보다 부진한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Negative(부정적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 산업별 수익률 전
망은 해당 산업 내 분석대상 종목들에 대한 담당 애널리스트의 분석과 판단에 따릅니다.

## Document 217: Entering the age of the older consumer As America ages, older consumers have more leisure time, significant wealth and growing economic influence. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Entering the age of the older consumer As America ages, older consumers have more leisure time, significant wealth and growing economic influence. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13023135     1

Economy 
Entering the age of the older consumer 
01 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• The older consumer is moving center stage. America's population has been aging for decades, but the shift has accelerated: the 
share aged over 60 increased by almost 10 percentage points (pp) between 1995 and 2025. And the Census Bureau projects the 
share of the population over 60 will rise another 5pp by 2055, to stand at nearly 30% of the population. 
• More free time means a different spending mix. People aged 65 and over spend around two and a half fewer hours per day 
working and roughly two hours more on leisure than the overall adult population. Bank of America data shows that older 
households allocate relatively more of their card spending to groceries and travel, and less to restaurants, gasoline, general 
merchandise and clothing. 
• Households aged over 55 held close to $140 trillion in net worth in the second quarter of 2026, giving many older consumers 
considerable capacity to spend on travel and other leisure categories. But wealth is not evenly distributed, and healthcare and 
other cost pressures remain significant for older households on tighter or fixed incomes.

Population aging is nothing new, but it is accelerating  
The US population has been growing relatively “older” for a long time. Exhibit 1 shows that the proportion of the population 
under 20 years old shrank from the mid-1960s through 2025. At the same time, the proportion of the population in older age 
groups has been rising.  
Exhibit 1: Over time the US population has steadily aged  
Proportion of US population by age range (%)

Source: Human Mortality Database; Bank of America Institute calculations 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

### Page 2

I NSTI TUTE

Additionally, there has been a notable acceleration in the relative aging of the population in recent years. For example, between 
1995 and 2025, the proportion of the population aged over 60 rose by almost 10 percentage points (pp) to 25%.  
Several factors have driven this. First, fertility rates in the US, as in much of the world, have fallen sharply. This has slowed 
natural population growth and, by reducing the number of younger people relative to older cohorts, has increased the share of 
the population at older ages. Second, more recently, net inward migration to the US has slowed. Because migrants tend to be 
younger than the overall population, weaker immigration also tends to raise the average age. Finally, rising life expectancy over 
much of the post-war period has allowed more people to survive into older age, further increasing the older population's share.  
Exhibit 2: The trend towards an older composition of the population is expected to continue  
Forecast changes in the share of population by age group relative to 2025 (pp)

Source: United States Census Bureau 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Lower fertility and, more recently, weaker net migration look set to slow overall US population growth in the decades ahead. As a 
result, older Americans are likely to play an increasingly important role in the economy, both as a share of the population and, in 
many age groups, in absolute numbers. The Census Bureau projects the share of the population over 60 will rise another 5pp by 
2055, to stand at nearly 30% of the population (Exhibit 2). For businesses, understanding how the needs and spending patterns 
of older consumers differ from those of younger generations will therefore become increasingly relevant. 
Older age groups allocate their time differently  
Why does this matter? One reason is that older people tend to spend their time and money quite differently from younger adults. 
Most obviously, many work less or have retired altogether. Data from the American Time Use Survey (ATUS) shows that people 
aged 65 and over spend, on average, around two and a half fewer hours per day working than the population aged 15+ (Exhibit 
3). 
That leaves considerably more time for other activities. People aged 65 and over spend around two hours more per day on 
leisure and sports activities, with television viewing accounting for a particularly large share of this additional leisure time 
(Exhibit 4).

Older households also spend differently  
Bank of America internal data on credit and debit card spending provides insights into how these differences in older 
generations’ use of time also impact their spending. Rather than splitting our spending data by generation, as we often do, here 
we split by age bands, allowing for consistent comparisons over time.  
Exhibit 5 shows how the composition of card spending of households aged 61-75 and those older than 75 differs from that of all 
households. A few things stand out. For one, older households devote a greater share of their overall card spending to groceries 
(food and beverages). Households aged 61-75 also allocate more spending to travel (airlines and lodging). And restaurants and 
bars, gasoline, general merchandise and clothing account for a smaller share of older households’ card spending.  
These differences may seem fairly intuitive. Older households have more leisure hours, so spend relatively more on travel to 
enjoy them. Previously we found that more available time may support travel particularly around retirement, although the 
discretionary spending lift can be temporary (for more, read our publication: Paychecks to pensions: The evolution of retiree 
spending).  
Older households are also likely to have less need to update their work wardrobes, so they probably spend less on clothing.

Still, older households’ relatively lower spending on restaurants and bars is a little more curious, perhaps, given dining out could 
be one way of making use of more leisure time. Indeed, when we look at how these spending shares are evolving, we find a 
nuanced picture. While households aged 61-75 do spend relatively less on restaurants and bars than the overall population, they 
appear to be narrowing this gap. But for those older than 75 this is not the case. In our view, the pandemic may still be weighing 
on dining-out spending among this older age group (Exhibit 6).  
Exhibit 6: For restaurants and bars the story is mixed across older

of all households, although it remains below its 2019 level (Exhibit 7).

Aging presents opportunities, but also challenges 
In our view, the aging of the US population presents important opportunities for the economy and businesses. Older consumers 
have more leisure time and businesses that cater to these demands may therefore benefit. 
Moreover, some older consumers hold considerable amounts of net wealth and have been some of the biggest beneficiaries of 
recent rises in equities. Using data from the Federal Reserve, Exhibit 8 shows that as of the second quarter of 2026 the net 
worth of those households aged 55 and over was close to $140 trillion, around three-quarters of the total. And with equity 
markets rising strongly, these age groups have seen a more than 20% increase in their net worth over the past two years, 
potentially enhancing their spending power further.  
But an important caveat is that not all older consumers are in this position. Previous Institute work found evidence consistent 
with equity-market wealth effects being concentrated toward higher-income households (read our publication: Paychecks to 
pensions: The evolution of retiree spending). And Census Bureau research has found that around 14% of Social Security 
recipients aged over 65 rely on it for over 90% of their income. For older households without a wealth cushion, recent elevated 
cost pressures, like gasoline, will likely have put them under more significant financial strain. They will also be more dependent 
on cost-of-living (COLA) increases in Social Security to underpin their spending.  
And, more broadly, outside of card spending, healthcare costs are a much larger factor in older households’ budgets (Exhibit 9). 
The evolution of these costs going forward will be a key variable for older consumers’ budgets.  
Exhibit 8: A large and growing proportion of net worth is held by 
older cohorts 
Distribution of household wealth by age ($, trillions)

Source: Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 9: Healthcare is a significant cost for older households

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts.  
Durables spending is defined as spending on electronics, building materials, auto-related spending and furniture. Premium durables spending is 
based on a selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively higher value products. Conversely, value durables spending is based on a 
selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively lower value products.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

AGGREGATE ESTIMATES AND REVISIONS 
 26Q3 Y/Y earnings are expected to be 30.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the Y/Y earnings estimate is 26.8%. 
 Of the 13 companies in the S&P 500 that have reported earnings to date for 26Q3, 84.6% have reported earnings 
above analyst estimates. This compares to a long-term average of 67.7% and prior four quarter average of 81.5%. 
 26Q3 Y/Y revenue is expected to be 11.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the growth estimate is 11.2%. 
 76.9% of companies have reported 26Q3 revenue above analyst expectations. This compares to a long-term 
average of 63% and an average over the past four quarters of 76.8%. 
 For 26Q3, there have been 40 negative EPS preannouncements issued by S&P 500 corporations compared to 65 
positive EPS preannouncements. By dividing 40 by 65 the N/P ratio is 0.6 for the S&P 500 Index.  
 The forward four-quarter (26Q4– 27Q3) P/E ratio for the S&P 500 is 20.1. 
 During the week of Oct. 5, three S&P 500 companies are expected to report quarterly earnings.

There has been an increase in the share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 since the start of the quarter (to $773.2B 
from $760.7B). Seven of the eleven sectors have experienced downward revisions to estimates.

Since Aug. 1, the materials (-9.1%) and health care (-2.2%) sectors have recorded the highest percentage decreases 
in earnings, while the energy (16.3%) sector has recorded the highest percentage increase in earnings. Overall, share-
weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 1.6% since the start of the quarter.

Since Aug. 1, the health care (-1.7B) and materials (-1.5B) sectors have recorded the highest dollar-level decreases in 
earnings, while the information technology (8.3B) sector has recorded the highest dollar-level increase in earnings. 
Overall, expected share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 12.5B since the start of the quarter.

The estimated earnings growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q3 is 30.6%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth 
rate declines to 26.8%. The S&P 500 expects to see share-weighted earnings of $773.2B in 26Q3, compared to share-
weighted earnings of $591.9B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 504 constituents) in 25Q3. 
11 of the 11 sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to 25Q3. The Energy and 
Information Technology sectors have the highest earnings growth rates for the quarter, while the Consumer Staples 
sector has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q3. 
The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (114.7%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $55.1B in 26Q3, 
compared to earnings of $25.7B in 25Q3. 4 of the 5 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher earnings 
than a year ago. The Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing (445.6%) and Integrated Oil & Gas (116.1%) sub-industries have 
the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 25.1%. 
The Information Technology sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (66.5%) of any sector. It is expected 
to earn $256.1B in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $153.8B in 25Q3. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Semiconductors (135.6%) and Semiconductor Materials & 
Equipment (68.9%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, 
the growth rate declines to 25.3%. 
The Consumer Staples sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (3.2%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $33.7B 
in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $32.6B in 25Q3. 3 of the 10 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower 
earnings than a year ago. The Packaged Foods & Meats (-6.5%) and Household Products (-4.2%) sub-industries have 
the lowest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 6.4%.

STARMINE EARNINGS SURPRISE FORECAST
Looking forward at quarterly performance, we use StarMine’s SmartEstimate® from LSEG to determine which
companies in the S&P 500 are better poised to beat and miss earnings estimates. The SmartEstimate® is a weighted

average of analyst estimates, with more weight given to more recent estimates and more accurate analysts. Our
studies have shown that when the SmartEstimate® differs significantly from the consensus (I/B/E/S mean estimate),
the Predicted Surprise accurately predicts the direction of earnings surprises or further revisions 70% of the time.
When a significant Predicted Surprise for revenue is also present for the period, the accuracy improves to 78%.

StarMine ARM from LSEG is an analyst revisions stock ranking model, designed to predict future changes in analyst
sentiment. ARM incorporates more accurate earnings estimates through the SmartEstimate prediction service. ARM
region rankings scores companies by region on a scale of 1 to 100 where 100 represents the most bullish sentiment.
Over the next two weeks, 31 S&P 500 companies are expected to report earnings. Of these companies, three positive

surprises and five negative surprises are expected from S&P 500 companies reporting quarterly results.

## Document 286: Persistent sources of inflation and the RBNZ (2026-10-08)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Persistent sources of inflation and the RBNZ (2026-10-08).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-08
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1
Westpac New Zealand Economics
A lack of headroom: The RBNZ, oil prices and administered costs.
• New Zealand households have faced a run of
large cost increases in recent years related
to administered charges and parts of the
economy that are less exposed to competition,
such as electricity charges and local
council rates.
• Those cost increases have continued for an
extended period, making them hard for the
RBNZ to look through.
• This has also meant that the RBNZ had little

headroom to absorb upside inflation surprises
even before the recent spike in oil prices.
• We expect that there will be ongoing sizeable
increases in many of these costs. That is a
key reason why we expect domestic (non-
tradables) inflation will be higher than the
RBNZ expects over the longer-term.
• That ongoing firmness in domestic inflation
is also a reason why we expect the RBNZ
will ultimately need to raise the OCR by
more than they assumed in their September

policy Statement.
• But getting inflation down to target levels
in the face of an oil price spike and high
administered prices won’t be easy and involves
some tough trade-offs. Administered prices
tend to be less responsive to changes in
economic conditions. As a result, interest
rate hikes are likely to have a bigger impact
on parts of the economy that are not the main
drivers of inflation.
Inflation is high for reasons beyond
oil prices.
Inflation in New Zealand rose to 4.1% in the year to June,

the highest it’s been in three years. And it’s set to remain
above 3% until at least mid-2027.
Much of the recent strength in inflation has been due
to the spike in oil prices since the outbreak of war in the
Middle East. However, even before the spike in fuel costs,
inflation had been running hot for an extended period:
inflation has been above 3% since mid-2025, and it’s now
been above the RBNZ’s 2% target midpoint for five years.
That persistent firmness in inflation is particularly notable

as, based on RBNZ estimates, the New Zealand economy
has had a negative output gap since September 2024,
with softness in economic growth and a weak labour
market. Those conditions would typically be associated
with more moderate inflation than we’ve seen.

Looking beneath the surface, much of the strength
in inflation over the past year has been related to
administered costs, such as government charges, and
price increases in parts of the economy that are less
1 Oct 2026 | Satish Ranchhod, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5668 | +64 21 710 852 | satish.ranchhod@westpac.co.nz
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
Persistent inflation and the RBNZ.

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
exposed to competitive pressures. For instance, over the
past year, local council rates rose nearly 9%, electricity
prices rose by 12% and health insurance premiums rose
by 19%.
The less competitive nature of some of these sectors
means they aren’t very responsive to changes in interest
rates. Instead, they are most likely to behave in a “cost-
plus” manner. And in some cases, recent years have seen
large increases in operating costs that are now being

passed on to consumers.
These aren’t one-off cost rises the RBNZ can
look through.
Just as the RBNZ might look through a one-off spike
in oil prices, there’s a reasonable argument for looking
through a one-off rise in administered prices that pushes
up inflation. For instance, a one-off increase in vehicle
registration costs might boost inflation temporarily, but
isn’t something the RBNZ would respond to.
However, the issue here is that the increases we’re seeing

are not one-off price rises. We’ve seen large increases
spread across a range of administered costs for several
years now (see chart on page 4). That’s been reflected
in measures of core inflation, which strip out swings in
volatile items like fuel and instead track the underlying
trend in prices. For example, excluding the rise in food
and fuel prices, inflation in the year to June was 2.9%,
and it’s been running close to or above 3% for five years

now. That’s meant that the RBNZ had little headroom to
absorb upside inflation surprises even before the recent
spike in fuel costs.

We expect that there will be continued large increases
in some administered costs. For instance, in the case
of local council rates, many councils will need to fund
significant infrastructure spending over the coming

years. There have been efforts to limit the extent of those
increases, such as the Government’s cap on council
rates. However, that doesn’t change the underlying
reason for the large rates increases in recent years, such
as increased spending on essential infrastructure. And if
councils can’t raise the required revenue through rates,
they may have to look at other approaches like user
pays. That could shift costs for households, rather than
reducing them.

Why does this matter?
It’s important to remember that the RBNZ’s focus is not

individual prices in the CPI (like fuel prices or council
rates), but the overall level of inflation. In essence, it
doesn’t matter why inflation is elevated – all that matters
is that it is elevated and how persistent inflation will be.
Right now, with core inflation already elevated, it’s hard
for the RBNZ to look through the large and continuing
cost increases that we’ve seen across a range of areas
that are less exposed to competition. And given the risk

of ongoing large increases in these costs, we expect that
domestic (non-tradables) inflation will be higher than the
RBNZ has assumed over the longer-term.
That’s part of the reason why we continue to expect that
the RBNZ will need to raise the Official Cash Rate by
more than they had assumed in their September policy
Statement. We’re forecasting a 25bp rate rise at the
RBNZ’s December meeting and expect the OCR will peak
at 4% next year. That risk is being compounded by the

protracted nature of the current rise in oil prices, along
with other risks such as El Niño which could boost food
prices over the coming year.
However, costs like oil prices, electricity charges, and
administered charges like council rates are not responsive
to changes in interest rates. As a result, when inflation
pressures are centred on such areas, the RBNZ needs
to work harder to keep overall inflation in check. It does
this by leaning against inflation in areas of the economy

that are more responsive to interest rates to bring
aggregate inflation pressures down towards target in a
timely manner. But rather than leaning against the source
of inflation pressures, the impact of tighter policy will
be to dampen demand and price-setting in other more

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
interest rate-sensitive parts of the economy such as the
construction sector or discretionary retail spending.
This is an uncomfortable but necessary trade-off. Tighter
policy will weigh on discretionary spending, the labour
market and near-term growth, and would affect prices in
parts of the economy that are not the drivers of inflation.
A higher OCR also means increases in households’ debt-
servicing costs, and for some households, that will be

very challenging.
However, looking through or not responding to these
sorts of ongoing large cost increases would still leave
households dealing with significant increases in overall
living costs. In the long run, that could be more damaging
for households and the economy.

Forecasts
Adding to the RBNZ’s concerns about both the oil price
shock and continued large increases in administered
prices, inflation expectations are relatively elevated. In
fact, long-term inflation expectations in business and
consumer surveys are close to where they were in 2022
when inflation was much higher than today. Similarly,
firms consistently signal in business surveys that cost
pressures are strong and margins have been squeezed.
Business pricing intentions vs consumer prices

Softness in demand might be limiting some firms’ ability
to lift prices at the current time. However, when demand
eventually firms again, there’s a risk that businesses
expect continued high levels of inflation and try to
push through larger price rises (sometimes referred to

as high inflation expectations becoming ‘embedded’).
That means inflation could remain high even when the
immediate impact of temporary boosts to inflation, like
an oil price spike, fades. This is a key reason why it’s
important to get inflation back to 2% on a consistent
basis, rather than allowing inflation to run at levels closer
to the top of the RBNZ’s target band.
The RBNZ does have discretion in how aggressively it
responds to inflation, and that is where the source of

inflation matters. The RBNZ is likely to adjust the OCR
more gradually when inflation is related to less interest
rate-sensitive areas, like oil costs or government charges.
And when doing so, it does account for how changes in
the Official Cash Rate could affect the labour market and
economic activity. But even if the RBNZ responds more
gradually to inflation, it can’t ignore ongoing large cost
increases indefinitely. Eventually inflation needs to be

brought back to target.
See overleaf for a chart of how consumer prices have
changed over the past 25 years.

## Document 310: The end of the safety net_ Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers (2026-09-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The end of the safety net_ Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers (2026-09-28).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

The end of the safety net:  
Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

28 September 2026 
For several decades, financial markets and the wider economy benefited from an implicit 
safety net. If a slowdown or a downturn arrived, there was a monetary put from central 
banks, and a fiscal put from governments. The monetary put would see interest rates 
slashed and liquidity injected via QE. The fiscal put would see governments run larger 
deficits to cushion any downturn. These policy stabilisers smoothed out the business 
cycle, and supported asset valuations too. 
These policy interventions were increasingly normalised from the late-1980s, and became 
more aggressive with each passing cycle. This reached its peak during the Covid-19 
pandemic, when governments ran fiscal deficits unprecedented in peacetime, whilst 
many central banks expanded their balance sheets with huge asset purchases. 
But after an era of ever-bigger interventions, these old stabilisers now face major 
obstacles. On the fiscal side, policy is constrained today by sovereign yields at multi-year 
highs, alongside the highest debt-to-GDP ratios in several decades. Then on the monetary 
side, policy is constrained by persistent inflation that’s increasingly supply-driven. 
This represents a big shift from the last few decades, but it does echo previous regimes 
where the macroeconomic stabilisers were either blunted or left unused. A good example 
is the 1970s experience, when stagflation and supply shocks posed a dilemma to 
traditional policy tools. Even further back, the pre-WWI environment of the classical gold 
standard and balanced budget orthodoxy saw governments play a very different and more 
limited role in macroeconomic stabilisation. 
Looking forward, this environment means policymakers will have a much more difficult 
time stabilising the business cycle in the coming years. So absent a sharp fall in yields or a 
collapse in inflation, higher macro volatility will be here to stay.

### Page 2

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
1. Background: How the stabilisers used to work.
Economic policymakers have long used interventions to stabilise output. This
became increasingly fashionable after WWII, when Keynesian economics
established a more active use for fiscal policy to stimulate the economy in a
downturn. This meant budget deficits became a more regular feature of life, rather
than used solely for wartime emergencies.

It was from the late 1980s, however, that the extent of policy interventions became
increasingly sizeable. Indeed, it was here that the origins of a central bank “put” for
financial markets began, in the crisis of Black Monday in 1987. That was a huge
stock market crash, where the S&P 500 fell over 20% in a single day, marking the
biggest daily decline in its entire history. The size of the falls even went beyond the
Great Depression, raising fears of wider contagion, particularly if it were to spark a

liquidity crisis that caused financial institutions to fail. However, the Federal
Reserve responded by cutting rates and providing liquidity, offering a
macroeconomic stabiliser against the prospect of wider economic stress. This
offered support against a wider downturn, and the US economic expansion
continued until 1990.
Whilst the genesis of a central bank “put” came in 1987, the idea gained further
traction in the late-1990s, with the Asian financial crisis in 1997, followed by the

Russian financial crisis in 1998. Fears of an economic contraction mounted,
particularly after issues at Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) raised concerns
that the US wasn’t immune from these global pressures. So once again, the Fed
delivered three 25bp rate cuts pre-emptively, seeking to support the economy.
A few years later, the bursting of the dot com bubble in early 2000 set the stage for
yet further stimulus. As equity markets declined, the Fed began to cut rates from

early 2001, ultimately delivering 475bps of cuts that year that took rates from 6.5%
to just 1.75%. That was followed up by further rate cuts in 2002 and 2003, leaving
rates at 1% by mid-2003 into 2004. Moreover, the Bush tax cuts were also delivered
in 2001 and 2003, acting as a fiscal stimulus as the wider US economy entered
recession.
In hindsight, these moves seem modest compared to what then happened in 2008,
when the Global Financial Crisis led to even bigger policy interventions. Suddenly,

the world faced the biggest economic contraction since the Great Depression, with
genuine fears that a repeat might happen. To avoid this, policymakers launched into
the biggest economic stimulus ever seen in a peacetime environment. That
included massive fiscal stimulus, with the US federal deficit at 9.8% of GDP in 2008,
the highest since 1945. Moreover, central banks slashed rates to the zero lower
bound, and many like the Federal Reserve launched into Quantitative Easing

programmes, purchasing assets to ease financial conditions even more. Over the
coming years, many began pledging to keep rates low well into the future,
pioneering the concept of forward guidance.
These levels of policy intervention weren’t confined to the immediate crisis
however. In the subsequent years, active attempts were made to prolong and
extend the business cycle, particularly as the 2010s became an era of low inflation
and low interest rates. In this environment, borrowing was historically cheap, and

low inflation meant central banks could cut rates without seeing a big overshoot of
their targets. Indeed, both the Fed and the ECB undershot their 2% inflation target
on average in the 2010s, and the ECB took their own deposit rate into negative
territory in 2014.

### Page 3

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3

By 2019, the year before the pandemic, the intellectual climate had continued to
move in the direction of more policy intervention. Government bond yields hit new

lows, with Germany’s 10-year bund yield at -0.7% in summer 2019. This
environment of negative yields meant borrowing was basically a “free lunch”, and
that summer also saw the Fed begin a 75bp easing cycle, as inflation was below
their target and they wanted to sustain the expansion.
The peak of this policy interventionism was seen with the Covid-19 pandemic in
2020. Just as in 2008, the world was facing a huge and sudden economic
contraction. Moreover, the consensus among policymakers was that they could

have done more to speed up the recovery after 2008, potentially avoiding years of
very high unemployment. As such, 2020 saw the most aggressive economic
stimulus packages ever seen in a peacetime environment, well above those in 2008.
Central banks immediately slashed rates back to the zero lower bound, pledged to
keep rates low for years to come, and launched into major QE programmes again.
Then on the fiscal side, record peacetime deficits were run. Indeed, the US federal

deficit of 14.5% of GDP in 2020 was the highest since 1945.

### Page 4

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
During the pandemic period, many initially expected these interventions to remain
for years to come. After all, in mid-2020, financial markets continued to price a
continuation of the low inflation and low interest rates of the 2010s. This belief was
clear on several fronts, and the 10-year Treasury yield closed at just 0.51% in August
2020. Meanwhile, the Federal Reserve even adjusted its own framework to allow for

average inflation targeting, i.e. permitting an overshoot of the inflation target after
periods of an undershoot. This reflected the consensus view that inflation was
unlikely to be a serious threat.
But as we now know, inflation returned in a serious way after the pandemic, forcing
central banks into rate hikes. Meanwhile, public debt-to-GDP ratios rose
significantly thanks to the pandemic stimulus, helping to push up sovereign bond
yields too. In other words, many of the usual policy tools of the previous few

decades were either blunted or no longer available.
2. The Monetary Constraint: High inflation means the central
bank put isn’t available
In the pre-2020 world described above, central banks regularly grappled with how
to generate enough inflation. In part, this was thanks to several structural forces
that put downward pressure on global prices. For instance, deepening
globalisation acted as a positive supply shock that kept costs down. Meanwhile, the

expansion of the global labour force in these years also boosted the supply side. In
other words, inflation was low and central banks generally had the space to cut
interest rates when they needed to.
But since the pandemic, we have entered a very different environment of above-
target inflation across many of the world’s biggest economies. Initially, that was
caused by a huge wave of pent-up demand after the pandemic, alongside
disruption to supply chains and labour markets. But the subsequent years also

brought further shocks. Those included Russia’s invasion of Ukraine in early 2022,
driving a big spike in European energy prices, and pushing Euro Area inflation above
10% briefly. In 2026, the closure of the Strait of Hormuz then acted as a fresh energy
shock, pushing inflation further away from target and contributing to a fresh
tightening cycle from the major central banks.
Figure 4: In the 2020s, inflation has generally been above central bank targets

(PCE inflation for the US, HICP inflation for the Euro Area)

### Page 5

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Alongside those shocks, the structural forces keeping inflation down have gone
into reverse. For instance, demographic trends have seen working-age populations
shrink in many places. Meanwhile, the trend towards deeper globalisation has
stalled, and the focus has turned to near-shoring and supply-chain resilience, rather
than locating production in the cheapest place. Simultaneously, other trends like

the push for higher defence spending are also adding to demand-side pressures.
Because of all this, the 2020s so far have brought persistently above-target inflation,
and central bankers have found it much more difficult to ease policy in response to
growth wobbles or financial stability fears.
This initially became apparent in 2022, amidst the biggest inflation wave for several
decades. Central banks had to keep hiking rates, even in the face of slowing growth

and financial market stress. Indeed, despite the S&P 500 being in bear market
territory by mid-2022, the Federal Reserve still launched into a series of 75bp hikes,
the biggest hikes since the 1990s. Then in 2023, despite the collapse of Silicon
Valley Bank and fears of wider financial contagion, the Fed and ECB still continued
with their rate hikes to combat inflation.
That tightening cycle in 2022-23 was a significant moment, because high inflation

meant monetary policy could not simply act as a stabiliser against growth or
financial stress. After all, policymakers realised that if they didn’t act to tackle high
inflation, they risked a repeat of the 1970s and the potential for even harsher
monetary tightening to get inflation under control again.
Today in 2026, this is a familiar problem again. We are in a globally synchronised
hiking cycle, with the Fed, ECB and Bank of Japan all having delivered rate hikes.

So far at least, global growth has been robust and financial conditions remain
accommodative by historical standards. But because inflation remains clearly
above target, central banks don’t have the space they did in the pre-pandemic world
to simply cut rates at the first sign of difficulty. There is still an inflation mandate to
be achieved, and given we have now seen multiple years of elevated inflation, there
is a much higher risk of inflation expectations becoming unanchored in that

context, particularly if another shock occurred.
3. The Fiscal Constraint: High yields and high public debt
mean fiscal stimulus isn’t easily available either
Just as monetary policy is constrained by high inflation, fiscal policy today is also
constrained by higher yields and higher public debt. This is a big transition from the
world of 1980-2020, when yields were generally falling throughout. Indeed, despite
all the fiscal stimulus around the GFC, the continuous decline in yields over the

2010s meant that debt dynamics remained manageable, because lower yields
helped to offset the impact of higher debt.
Since the pandemic however, we’ve been in a very different world, as the dual
forces of higher debt and higher yields have made debt dynamics far more
unfavourable around the world. And this isn’t just a nominal story, as real yields
have also risen to multi-year highs as well.

### Page 6

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG

In essence, there are 4 new constraints around fiscal stimulus.
First, is the significant rise in global yields. By definition, the cost of incurring debt
has grown, in both nominal and real terms. Relative to the pre-pandemic baseline

in early 2020, the 10-year US Treasury yield has risen from 1.9% to 5.2%. Similarly
in Germany, the 10-year bund yield has risen from -0.2% to 3.6%. Moreover, with
yields already elevated, any further borrowing risks crowding out the private sector,
and further dampening growth. And for governments, interest spending has
ramped up significantly. For instance, US net interest spending as a share of GDP
exceeded its recent peak from the early 1990s in the most recent fiscal year, and the

CBO forecast this is set to continue.
Figure 6: US Net Interest Costs (% of GDP) with CBO Forecasts

Source : CBO, Deutsche Bank
The second constraint is that public debt itself is now at a higher level. This interacts
with the higher yields mentioned above, because even though yields have only
returned to their levels from the mid-2000s, that is much more impactful because
they are being paid on a much larger debt pile. Using IMF numbers for consistency,

net public debt across the advanced economies has risen from 52% of GDP in 2000,
to 80% in 2025, with further increases projected to 2030. This means the fiscal
space available to governments is more limited than it used to be.

### Page 7

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
The third constraint on fiscal stimulus is the high starting level of budget deficits. So
to generate a meaningful fiscal stimulus, it would require deficits to be pushed up
to even higher levels than they are today. But that in turn would add to the risks of
bond market disruption that cascades through the economy, leading to higher
borrowing costs for corporates and consumers as well.
The fourth constraint is higher inflation. When inflation is high, any fiscal stimulus

that boosts demand risks pushing up prices rather than boosting output, and in turn
makes monetary tightening more likely that could offset the impact of the stimulus.
This trade-off was apparent in 2022, when central banks were hiking rates
aggressively, and even further back in the inflationary environment of the 1970s. In
a world of high inflation and supply constraints, fiscal stimulus is more likely to
overheat the economy.
4. Are there any other eras of history where the stabilisers

weren’t available?
Whilst history never quite repeats exactly, there have been other eras in which the
traditional policy stabilisers were either unavailable or severely blunted, which hold
some lessons for our own time.
The pre-WWI classical gold standard
Before WWI, the international monetary system was known as the gold standard,
in which currencies were convertible into a fixed quantity of gold. In practice
however, this acted as a huge constraint on macroeconomic stabilisation. After all,

monetary policy had to maintain the convertibility of gold at a fixed parity. This
meant contemporary options like QE weren’t available in a crisis. Meanwhile on the
fiscal side, governments generally adhered to balanced budget orthodoxy. This was
before Keynes wrote the General Theory, so the modern understanding of
countercyclical deficit spending was not in place. In the US, there wasn’t even a
central bank until the Federal Reserve’s establishment in 1913.

Figure 7: Gold Prices (US Dollars per ounce, log scale)

### Page 8

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Without modern policy stabilisers however, this was also an era of huge
macroeconomic volatility. There was a major financial panic across Europe and the
US in 1873, which marked the start of the so-called “Long Depression”. In the US,
that contraction lasted until 1879, which in length was even longer than the Great
Depression contraction of 1929-33. And this was followed by other major panics,

including the 1893 panic that led to another US contraction, along with the 1907
panic that saw the Dow Jones almost halve relative to its 1906 peak.
The stagflationary 1970s
The more recent example comes from the 1970s, another era of multiple supply
shocks. The biggest were the two oil shocks in 1973 and 1979, which both
represented negative supply shocks that pushed up inflation and hurt growth in the
US and Europe. However, this meant policymakers faced a dilemma. If central

banks cut rates, that would add to the inflationary pressures and risk worsening the
wage-price spirals already underway. Meanwhile, if fiscal stimulus were pursued,
that would also push up inflation.
Figure 8: US CPI (year-on-year % change)
-2
0

1955 1960 1965 1970 1975 1980 1985
First Oil Shock
Second Oil Shock
Source : Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank
This context meant that policy couldn’t smooth out the business cycle as
successfully as it had in other decades. Indeed, in the decade from the first oil shock

in 1973, the US experienced 3 separate recessions. That is significantly more
turbulent than the last 40 years, when there have been just 4 recessions in total, or
once a decade on average. For financial markets too, the 1970s were a terrible
period for asset performance, with both equities and bonds losing ground in real
terms across multiple countries.
5. Implications
If economic policymakers can’t use traditional monetary and fiscal policy tools to

stabilise output and smooth the business cycle, then this will result in higher macro
volatility.
If we look back at the last 40 years, it is notable that the 5 most recent US expansions
all rank in the top 7 longest expansions since business-cycle dating began. This has
several causes, and some of it reflects a move away from an agriculture-based
economy where output was dependent on factors like the weather. But a key role
has also been played by pre-emptive policy interventions, such as cutting rates to

stop a slowdown turning into a recession. Indeed, recent examples include the a
dovish shifts in monetary policy after the 1987 stock market crash, the 1997-98

### Page 9

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
financial crises, and the mid-cycle slowdown of 2015-16.
Beyond the actual policy interventions, these moves also had a confidence effect
that supported financial markets. The fact that policymakers stood ready to take
action helped create a virtuous circle, as optimism on the economic outlook
boosted financial asset values, supported wealth effects, and avoided a tightening
in financial conditions that would itself cause growth to slow.

Source : NBER, Deutsche Bank
Clearly, policy is not as constrained today as in some periods of history. There is no

longer a gold standard where the convertibility needs to be maintained. And unlike
the 1970s, inflation is not in double-digits. Nevertheless, this is still a more
constrained world than we have been used to in the last 4 decades. Higher inflation
and more frequent supply shocks are constraining monetary policy. Meanwhile,
higher yields and higher public debt levels are constraining fiscal policy. We have
already seen the impacts since the pandemic, most notably in 2022, when central

banks hiked rates to combat the inflation surge, and there was a simultaneous
selloff across bonds and equities of a kind unseen in modern times.
Looking forward, this environment will offer new challenges of the sort we haven't
been used to in recent decades. The policy trade-offs are now more acute, and if
pre-emptive stabilisation is no longer available, then higher macro volatility lies
ahead.

Source: U.S. Department of Commerce, U.S. Department of Labor, IHS Markit, Federal Reserve Board and Wells Fargo Economics
Sector Analysis
Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE)
• Consumer spending is holding up, and we expect real PCE to rise at around a 3.0% annualized rate
in Q3.
Consumer spending has been mixed, yet resilient in recent months. After a soft July, driven largely by

weaker durable goods purchases, retail sales rebounded strongly in August, rising at a 1.2% monthly
clip amid broadly distributed gains across retailers. The latest data have led us to lift our near-term
growth forecast, and we now look for real PCE to rise at a 3.2% annualized clip in Q3. Previous data
will also likely be revised higher on firmer control group sales data and strong services revenue in Q2,
suggesting recent consumer momentum remains intact. All told, the consumer is still in decent shape

and has capacity to keep spending. Real income growth remains modest but is beginning to improve
as inflation eases and the labor market remains broadly supportive suggesting continued consumption
even if that pace of spending may moderate into year-end.
Investment: Equipment, Intellectual Property Products and Inventories
• The AI build-out is still king in terms of the contribution to business investment, but the
broadening in capex spending beyond the tech sector is taking root.

AI-related investment continues to dominate capital allocation. There are no signs of firms scaling
back their outlays now, though at some point as AI investment matures, merely sustaining spending
at elevated levels will provide a smaller incremental boost to growth than it has in recent quarters.
Beyond AI, the tentative signs we've identified that signal a broadening in the investment backdrop
are getting a bit louder. The ISM manufacturing index has been in expansion territory for eight-

Economics | 3

### Page 4

Monthly Economics
straight months, and durable goods orders have firmed across a wider range of industries. Part of
this improvement likely reflects delayed capital spending plans finally moving forward, but inventory
rebuilding after five consecutive quarters of drawdown may be lending some support as well.

Source: U.S. Department of Commerce and Wells Fargo Economics
Investment: Residential
• Residential construction looks likely to taper through the rest of the year while mortgage rates and
new home inventories remain elevated.
Headwinds have intensified against residential construction. The trend in single-family construction is
downshifting, emblematic of the affordability challenges depressing buyer demand. As of this writing,

mortgage rates have risen to over 7.0% and builder confidence has declined alongside. Sales stuck in
a low gear mean that new home listings are inching higher, further discouraging new development.
Multifamily construction appears to be holding up but faces limited potential upside with vacancy
rates elevated across much of the South. We still see a potential for marginal improvement in 2027,
but higher mortgage rates dampen our outlook.
Investment: Nonresidential Structures

• Construction appears to be gaining steam, led by data centers and power. However, sluggish
commercial starts elsewhere and high interest rates are likely to keep growth relatively modest.
The AI infrastructure build out is supporting modest growth in nonresidential construction. After
moderating for a full year, private nonresidential outlays rose from April to July. Data centers and
power are propelling current momentum. Though construction has also strengthened across sports

facilities and communication infrastructure, commercial projects remain stuck in low gear. Categories
like warehousing and retail remain soft, and overall architectural billings have improved only modestly.
Looking ahead, a recent breakout in megaprojects is likely to support additional data center and
manufacturing development, while higher interest rates limit overall growth.

Net Exports & Trade
• Net exports are tracking to slice nearly two-percentage points off headline GDP growth amid
strong imports.
Imports are shaping up for another strong quarter after a surge in July and a rebound in August
retail spending suggesting continued domestic demand for product. The July import gain was again

almost entirely driven by high-tech related goods like computers & accessories, semiconductors and
telecommunications equipment. We still anticipate a bit more of a broadening out in imports to come,
perhaps as early as August as businesses replenish inventory amid continued consumer demand.
Export growth is a bit more constrained, suggesting an expansion in the trade deficit and that net
exports will slice nearly two-percentage points off headline GDP growth in Q3.

Labor Market
• We modestly raised our 2026 payroll forecast and made no material changes to our 2027 forecast.
We continue to expect the unemployment rate to end this year at 4.2%.
August's employment report reinforced that the labor market remains stable. Payroll growth has
averaged 106K over the past six months, though we expect some moderation in the months ahead.
Hiring measures from regional Fed surveys and hiring plans from small businesses have cooled from

their early summer jumps and job openings have leveled off. At the same time, initial jobless claims
remain near multi-decade lows and continue to indicate that layoffs remain contained. We expect
payroll growth to moderate to roughly 65K per month over the remainder of the year, a pace broadly
consistent with our estimate of breakeven. As hiring cools slightly and the participation rate rebounds
somewhat further, we see some scope for the unemployment rate to edge up to 4.2% by year-end.

Even with the slight increase though, labor market conditions would remain consistent with full
employment.
Inflation
• Incorporating our expectations for the upcoming BEA methodology changes and recent inflation
data, we look for the core PCE deflator to end the year at 3.2% on a Q4-over-Q4 basis, up from
3.1% in our previous forecast.
Headline inflation remains tied to the conflict in the Middle East, although underlying price pressures
continue to move in a more favorable direction. Higher oil prices are keeping some upward pressure

on energy-sensitive categories in the near term, but tariff-related goods inflation is fading and price
pressures outside a handful of technology-related categories remain limited. Meanwhile, core services
inflation remains on a moderating trend as shelter inflation continues to gradually cool and labor cost
pressures slowly ease. Taken together, inflation pressures appear less widespread than earlier this year,
Economics | 5

### Page 6

Monthly Economics
but recurring supply shocks are likely to keep the path back to 2% bumpy. We expect the core PCE
deflator to slow to 3.2% on a Q4-over-Q4 basis this year.

Fiscal Policy
• We forecast this year's federal budget deficit will total $2.1 trillion (~6.5% of GDP), and next year's
will be about the same in dollar terms and a bit smaller as a share of GDP (~6%).

The federal budget deficit continues to hover in the 6.0-6.5% of GDP range, similar to where it was
at the end of the Biden administration. The outlook for the next couple of years is one of status quo.
We doubt there will be any new, big fiscal legislation with divided government likely after the midterm
elections, but rising interest costs and steady growth in mandatory spending programs such as Social
Security and Medicare will keep deficits from shrinking much. It's not just U.S. fiscal problems putting

upward pressure on long-term rates. Other factors such as robust corporate debt issuance, policy
uncertainty and foreign factors are also playing a role. Our base case for 6.0%-6.5% of GDP budget
deficits will not help when it comes to bringing down long-term interest rates.
6 | Economics

### Page 7

U.S. Economic Outlook: September 2026 Economics
Monetary Policy & Interest Rates
• We have changed our fed funds forecast to 4.00-4.25% by year-end 2026, with no changes in
2027. Our year-end forecasts for the 10-year Treasury yield are also higher at 4.90% and 4.75%,
respectively, for this year and next.
The FOMC made clear at its September 16 meeting that stubbornly high PCE inflation this year was
the key factor behind the Committee's decision to raise rates. Given this, we think only a string of

good inflation data can bail the Fed out, and accordingly we think one more rate hike is coming this
year, with softer inflation data in 2027 giving way to a more patient FOMC. We could easily envision
one more hike beyond that or a "one and done" hike where the FOMC does not go again. Outcomes
outside that range, however, strike us as unlikely. A rising neutral rate makes meaningful rate cuts hard
to justify in the absence of an economic slowdown, while the case for a more aggressive tightening

cycle seems weak given that the labor market is not overheating and much of the excess inflation is
supply side driven. We think a very short gradual tightening cycle and an eventual reduction in energy
prices/slowdown in inflation will allow Treasury yields to drift modestly lower in 2027. But, the factors
mentioned above (e.g., large budget deficits, rising neutral rate) should prevent yields from falling in a
bigger way.

International Developments
• Global growth has proved more resilient than expected, prompting us to upgrade our 2026 global
GDP growth forecast to 2.9%. We have also modestly revised our global inflation outlook higher,
lifting our 2026 CPI forecast to 4.4% and our 2027 forecast accordingly.
• We now expect more tightening across advanced economies than in our previous monthly forecast
update, while emerging market policy paths remain mixed.

Global economic activity proved more resilient than expected in H1-2026, with early indicators
suggesting that momentum carried into Q3. While precautionary stockpiling supported some of the
earlier strength, activity has remained resilient on firmer services, continued AI-related investment and
exports (U.S., South Korea, Japan and Australia) and increased defense spending (Eurozone and Japan).
We expect inflation to remain higher for longer before easing next year and averaging 4% in 2027 as

tighter financial conditions weigh on demand and supply pressures fade. Risks remain skewed to the
upside. Oil and product inventories remain below pre-conflict levels, while limited refining capacity and
ongoing disruptions leave energy prices vulnerable to renewed pressure. El Niño and higher fertilizer
costs threaten food prices, while AI-related demand is raising semiconductor costs. Second-round
effects remain limited, but successive supply shocks increase the risk of persistent inflation into next

year.

Understanding the balance
ﬁlm industry for a large AI
chip (Part II)
上次我們提到  balance ﬁlm 產業  101 ，這次來談談替代技術，產業
鏈核⼼競爭⼒⽐較，測算市場規模  TAM
ANDREW LU ON GLOBAL SEMI/TECHS
OCT 1 ∙ PAID
READ IN APP
Executive summary

上期從技術的⾓度解釋何謂  Balance Film ，何時市場將採⽤，三個原因為何
⼤ AI 晶⽚需要它，貼在哪裡，移除之後翹曲回歸怎辦（Understanding the
balance ﬁlm industry for a larger AI chip (Part I)）， Part II 我們想談談
有沒有更優的防翹曲技術，產業鏈玩家的核⼼競爭⼒⽐較，如何測算市場規模
及營收貢獻，及產業鏈⾵險。
4 better technology than balance ﬁlm -emerging
risks?
要完全取代製程中的  Balance Film ，核⼼邏輯必須從「事後加膜反向對沖應
⼒」轉變為「在源頭消除熱應⼒」或「由機台與設計端主動消化形變」。現階
段  Balance Film 依然是  FOPLP 與⼤尺⼨晶⽚封裝中最快⾒效、改裝成本最
低的利器。但隨著新技術低溫介電材料成熟與局部雷射接合成為先進封裝標準
配備，確實對暫時性應⼒平衡膜的市場有可能會產⽣結構性的變化，我們以後

會持續關注其進展及盲點突破。
1. 低溫硬化與超低  CTE 介電材料（從材料源頭降溫）
熱應⼒的物理公式為（熱膨脹係數差  x 降溫溫差）。傳統製程需要  Balance
Film ，主因是光阻與介電層（如傳統  PI 、 PBO ）需要⾼達  230°C–250°C 的
⾼溫烘烤固化，冷卻回室溫產⽣龐⼤溫差。

### Page 2

低溫硬化光敏介電材料（ Low-Temp Cure Dielectrics ）： 杜邦、旭
化成與東京應化等開發硬化溫度降⾄  150°C–180°C 的新⼀代光阻 / 介電
材，⼤幅縮減溫差，使基板在冷卻後的累積收縮應⼒直接減少  40% 以
上。
超低  CTE 樹脂配⽅： 透過奈⽶級無機⼆氧化矽（ Silica ）⾼充填技術，
將有機介電層的  CTE 壓低⾄接近矽（ 3–4 ppm/°C ）或玻璃（ 6–8 ppm/
°C ），使材料間不再因熱脹冷縮相互扯動。
新技術的盲點：硬化溫度從  250°C 降⾄  150°C–180°C ，代價是分⼦鏈
交聯密度（ Cross-linking Density ）急遽下降。這會直接導致玻璃轉移
溫度降低、耐化性變差。後續進⼊強酸鹼的微影蝕刻與電鍍銅製程時，材
料極易被藥⽔侵蝕膨脹，導致層間脫層（ Delamination ）。⽽為了降低
CTE ，材料商必須強⾏摻⼊  60%–80% 的奈⽶⼆氧化矽（ Silica ）填
料。這會使樹脂黏度爆表、流動性喪失，可能無法填平超細微線路，並造

成介電損耗⼤幅上升。
付費訂閱戶可分享四種防翹曲新技術及其盲點，全球
balance ﬁlm 產業鏈核⼼競爭⼒差異，台積電會⽤
嗎？平衡膜 TAM 市場預測
Subscribed
2. 雷射輔助接合（ LAB, Laser-Assisted Bonding ）
傳統覆晶（ Flip-Chip ）或熱壓合（ TCB ， Thermal Compression Bonding ）
是將整塊矽載板與晶粒放⼊⾼溫迴銲爐或整體加熱平台，引發⼤⾯積基板與
EMC 的劇烈熱膨脹。
局部精準加熱： LAB 利⽤均勻擴束的紅外雷射，在數百毫秒（ ms ）內僅
對晶⽚下⽅微凸塊（ Micro-bump ）區域進⾏瞬時加熱融焊。
載板維持冷態： 下⽅的⼤尺⼨玻璃或⾯板載板本體溫度始終維持在  80°C
以下，不觸發⼤規模熱膨脹與收縮，從根本上消除了⾯板因迴銲溫度衝擊
⽽翹曲的成因。
新技術的盲點：多晶⽚超⼤⾯積下的能量均勻度極限：  雷射光束要在數
百毫秒內均勻覆蓋如  Blackwell 等級  GPU （ >1600mm2 ）甚⾄更⼤的

雙晶⽚複合體，其光場能量均勻度（ Beam Uniformity ）極難控制在
+/-2% 以內。⽽且  LAB 是由上⽅照光，但⾯對  12Hi / 16Hi 的  HBM 記
憶體時，垂直熱阻極⾼。雷射能量傳遞⾄最底層  Micro-bump 熔錫時，
頂層矽晶粒早已過熱受損；若降低雷射功率，底層則形成冷焊虛焊
（ Cold Solder Joint ）。最後就是可能的毫秒級熱衝擊（ Thermal
Shock ），  瞬時加熱與驟冷會在晶粒與中介層之間形成極陡峭的暫態溫度

### Page 3

梯度，微凸塊內部瞬間累積巨⼤的剪切應⼒，在後續的溫度循環測試中極
易脆裂。
3. 多分區主動靜電吸盤與⾼景深曝光機（設備端強制平整）
過去微影（ Lithography ）步驟必須依賴  Balance Film 維持基板平整，是因
為曝光機的景深範圍（ DOF, Depth of Focus ）極⼩（僅數微⽶），⼀旦⾯板
微翹，光阻就會失焦。
動態分區靜電吸盤（ Multi-zone ESC ）： 新⼀代  FOPLP 曝光設備（如
EVG 、 SUSS MicroTec ）導⼊⾼剛性陶瓷、具備數百個微氣道獨⽴調控
的靜電吸盤，能在真空下直接以物理吸⼒強制將變形載板拉平⾄極限公差
內。
即時表⾯測⾼與動態調焦（ In-situ Height Mapping ）： 曝光系統在
步進掃描時即時偵測⾯板三維微型凹凸，鏡頭以奈秒級補償追焦，直接消
化載板的物理起伏，無需耗材輔助。
新技術的盲點：1. 脆性基板破碎⾵險（ Catastrophic
Shattering ）：  超薄玻璃載板（厚度僅  0.5–0.7mm ）屬於⾼度脆性材

料。多分區靜電吸盤（ Multi-zone ESC ）透過分區差異化施壓強⾏吸平
載板時，局部的剪應⼒集中（ Stress Concentration ）極易直接引發玻
璃碎裂。⼤尺⼨⾯板⼀旦在無塵室機台內碎裂，清機停線代價動輒數天起
跳。2. 應⼒釋放後的線路撕裂（ Springback & Trace
Cracking ）：  吸盤只能在「吸住的當下」維持物理⽔平。微⽶級銅導線
在被強制拉平的狀態下成形，⼀旦⾯板脫離吸盤移⾄下⼀道⼯序，應⼒瞬
間回彈，沉積在表⾯的微細銅線可能會因劇烈的壓縮 / 拉伸變形⽽直接被
震斷或剝離。
4. 改善玻璃核⼼基板的熱膨脹係數⼯程（ CTE-Tunable Glass ）
使⽤傳統有機基板或普通浮法玻璃時，載板與矽晶⽚、⾦屬銅導線的膨脹率落
差顯著。
客製化膨脹率玻璃： 康寧（ Corning ）與肖特（ SCHOTT ）針對次世代
玻璃基板開發出「可調式  CTE 配⽅」，能直接合成出  CTE 精準落在  3.2
到  3.5 ppm/°C（完全貼合單晶矽）的專⽤封裝玻璃。當核⼼載體與矽

晶粒的熱膨脹步調完全⼀致時，冷卻過程幾乎不產⽣內應⼒差，基板便無
需背⾯的防翹曲膜。
新技術的盲點：玻璃廠商（如  Corning 、 SCHOTT ）確實能將玻璃  CTE
調⾄  3.2 ppm/°C 完美匹配單晶矽。但封裝正⾯依然存在龐⼤體積的⾦
屬銅（ CTE ~16.5-17.0 ppm/°C ）與介電絕緣材料（ CTE >20 ppm/
°C ）。只要正⾯沉積了多層厚銅  RDL ，⾼溫冷卻後的單向收縮⼒矩依然無
處釋放。即便核⼼基板使⽤玻璃，正⾯厚銅依然會拖拽玻璃基板邊緣捲
起。最後就是為了降低玻璃  CTE ，成分中添加了特殊的⾦屬氧化物，這

### Page 4

會⼤幅惡化雷射對玻璃的吸收特性，導致玻璃通孔（ TGV ）雷射加⼯與化
學蝕刻良率銳減、孔壁粗糙度上升。
Global balance ﬁlm supply chain
抗翹曲平衡膜（ Balance Film / Warpage Control Film ）的供應商主要分為
兩⼤陣營：⻑期掌握⾼分⼦基礎材料專利的⽇系材料⼤廠，以及緊貼台積電、
⽇⽉光等先進封裝聚落，以⾼客製化與「材料＋設備綑綁」突圍的台系供應
鏈。
Source: Andrew Lu on global semis/techs
核⼼競爭⼒差異
1. 商業模式：⽇系的「材料規格定義」 vs. 台廠的「設備 + 材料  Bundle 」

### Page 5

⽇系⼤廠（ Nitto 、 Lintec ）：  核⼼競爭⼒在於⾼純度化學配⽅與極⾼的可靠
度認證。它們通常作為全球晶圓廠的標準耗材（ Standard Spec ），客戶製程
需配合其材料特性調整。
台廠突圍（⼭太⼠＋⾟耘）：  應⼒平衡在  FOPLP ⾯板級（如
510mm×515mm 以上）很難單靠材料解決。台廠採取「材料配⽅＋⾃動化
壓膜機台＋腔體壓⼒控制」的統包捆綁模式，能根據客戶的翹曲曲率
（ Smile/Cry 變形）即時微調膜材模數與貼合張⼒，解決超薄基板在製程搬運
時容易碎裂的痛點。
2. 技術路徑：暫時性對沖膜  vs. 永久性結構材料
製程對沖型（ Balance Film ，如⼭太⼠、亞電、晶化科技）：  屬於「製程暫
時性輔助材料」。重點在於⾼溫固化時產⽣精確的反向收縮⼒，且在⾼溫  RDL
或電鍍完成後，必須能乾淨解膠（ Debonding ）⽽不殘膠、不傷線路。
本體改良型（如  Resonac ）：  核⼼技術在於調配低  CTE 、低固化收縮率的
EMC 或絕緣介電層，從源頭降低熱應⼒。但在異質整合（玻璃 + 銅 + 晶粒）的

極限物理特性下，結構材料難以完全消除應⼒差，這為暫時性  Balance Film
創造了不可或缺的市場空間。
3. 晶圓級（ WLP ）與⾯板級（ PLP ）的製程轉移優勢
⽇系⼤廠在  12 吋晶圓（圓形）擁有絕對的市佔率與設備相容性。然⽽當輝
達、超微推動先進封裝⾛向⼤⾯積矩形（ FOPLP 或玻璃載板）時，傳統圓形
貼膜機台⾯臨邊⾓應⼒不均與裁剪損耗問題。台系供應鏈因具備⾯板雙虎（友
達、群創）轉型  FOPLP 的地緣合作優勢，在⼤尺⼨矩形應⼒平衡膜的客製打
樣與驗證節奏上，展現出顯著的敏捷度。
Will TSMC use balance ﬁlm?
鎖定在抗翹曲平衡膜（ Balance Film / Warpage Control Film ）這項利基材
料上，真正有機會切⼊台積電先進封裝（ CoWoS-L 、 CoPoS 、 InFO 及次世代
⾯板級 / 玻璃載板）供應鏈的廠商，主要可分為四⼤核⼼要⾓。⽬前看來，⽇系
⼤廠多著重「標準化晶圓（圓形）產品」，⾯對台積電客製化⽐例極⾼的

CoWoS-L 巨型載板、或是矩形玻璃基板等⾮標準需求，⽇廠的客製打樣與配
⽅調整週期較⻑。這給了本⼟廠商以敏捷性搶下新型  Balance Film 的機會，
但就最近的產業鏈調研來看，台積電短期內採⽤  Balance Film 的時機還沒來
到，我們估計可能要等到 2028 年以後  。
1. ⼭太⼠  (AMC)
切⼊定位：  CoWoS 、 CoPoS （ Panel 級先進封裝）與玻璃基板抗翹曲平衡
膜。

### Page 6

已通過驗證並放量：  ⼭太⼠早期以光學膜起家，轉型後開發的低應⼒
Balance Film 成功解決⼤尺⼨多晶⽚堆疊時的嚴重翹曲問題，⽬前仍與台積
電進⾏測試及技術驗證，但量產時間仍未定。
設備與材料綑綁（ Bundle ）綜效：  應⼒調控在⾯板級（ Panel-level ）和⼤
尺⼨晶圓上難以單靠貼膜解決。⼭太⼠與濕製程 / 壓膜設備⼤廠⾟耘
（ Scientech ）深度結盟，提供「配⽅膜材＋⾃動化壓膜機台」⼀體化⽅案，
極⼤降低了台積電產線試產的調整期與破碎⾵險。
CoPoS / 玻璃載板先發地位：  隨著矽中介層光罩尺⼨將突破  9x ，台積電即
將於 2029 年推進矩形基板（ CoPoS ，請參考之前圓轉⽅深度報告：How
TSMC's CoPoS to beneﬁt glass interposer substrate makers?），⼭太
⼠的  Balance Film 因轉換成本極⾼、具備主供優勢，被視為台積電⼤尺⼨封
裝藍圖中的關鍵本⼟材料⽀柱。
2. 晶化科技  (WaferChem) ：專攻  InFO / CoWoS 的台系專利突圍者

切⼊定位：  晶圓翹曲調控膜（ Warpage Control Film ）、雷射解膠膜。
直指台積電  3D IC 痛點：  晶化科技是台灣少數純⾃主開發出晶圓級翹曲調控
膜的特⽤材料廠，已成為台灣及中國⼤陸三⼤封測廠供應商，並專⾨針對台積
電  InFO （扇出型封裝）與  CoWoS 研發，強調能在背側加溫固化、主動調控
翹曲度，以利後續多層微細  RDL ⾛線。除了  balance ﬁlm 之外，晶化還有
TBF build-up ﬁlm ( 與 Ajinomoto ABF 產品類似）， molding ﬁlm, laser
debond ﬁlm, die attach ﬁlm ，反⽽可能⽐單純的  balance ﬁlm 公司更有
系統級優勢。
⾼溫與重⼯（ Rework ）特性：  其膜材主打耐溫達  300°C 以上且具備  UV/ 熱
解膠與  Rework 能⼒。雖然出貨規模尚不及⼀線⼤廠，但在台積電極⼒扶植
「特⽤材料在地化（ Local Sourcing ）」的政策下，晶化科技具備⾼度⼆階

（ Second-source ）的驗證機會。
3. ⽇東電⼯  (Nitto Denko) & 琳得科  (Lintec) ：既有標準製程的防守者
切⼊定位：  12 吋晶圓研磨、暫時鍵合解膠與既有  CoWoS-S/R 薄膜標準品。
歷史綁定深厚：  台積電既有的晶圓級製程保護膜、 UV 解膠膜⻑年由  Nitto 與
Lintec 寡占，兩者擁有龐⼤的⾼分⼦合成專利與無塵塗佈產能。但技術能⼒雖

## Document 344: Why the US rare earth supply chain remains vulnerable

Source PDF: all report pdf/Why the US rare earth supply chain remains vulnerable.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-05
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The US has signiﬁcant rare earth resources, but its supply chain
remains heavily reliant on China. The biggest gaps do not sit in
the mine, but rather in processing, heavy rare earth separation
and magnet manufacturing

Ewa Manthey and Coco Zhang
A rare earths mine in Mountain Pass, California
The US supply chain remains incomplete
Last year we wrote about how China could use its dominance of
rare earth supply chains as political leverage. Although supply
pressures eased after the US and China reached a framework
agreement in June 2025, Beijing's subsequent expansion of rare
earth export controls in October highlighted the persistent
vulnerability of downstream supply chains.
Headline import dependence tells only part of the story. The US is
producing more rare earths, but it still cannot process enough of
that output into the specialised materials and magnets needed by

domestic industry. The main weakness emerges after the ore
leaves the mine.
US rare earth production is growing, but
China still leads

Resource availability is not the main
challenge for the US

### Page 3

Source: USGS, ING Research
Mining is only the ﬁrst step
Once ore is mined, it must be concentrated and separated into
individual oxides. These are then converted into metals and alloys
before being manufactured into magnets. China dominates these
value-added stages.
Mountain Pass shows both the strength and weakness of the US
position. MP Materials produced a record 50,692 tonnes of rare
earth oxide in concentrate in 2025 and expanded production of

separated neodymium-praseodymium oxide. The company also
began manufacturing neodymium-iron-boron magnets in Texas in
December 2025.
These are important steps towards a domestic mine-to-magnet
supply chain. But one mine, increasing light rare earth separation
and limited magnet production, cannot yet supply the full range of
products needed by US industry.
The US rare earth capacity weakens further
down the supply chain
Cookie settings

### Page 4

Source: ING Research
The real choke points are downstream
US vulnerability increases further down the chain. According to the
International Energy Agency (IEA), China accounts for 60% of
mined magnet rare earths, 91% of reﬁned output and 94% of
permanent magnet production. Two decades ago, it produced only
about half of the world’s permanent magnets.
Mining capacity is growing in the US, but China retains an
overwhelming advantage where most of the value is added. That

position was built over decades through state support, integrated
supply chains, technical expertise and proximity to downstream
manufacturers. Together, these factors created economies of scale
that are diﬃcult for new entrants to replicate.
Separating individual rare earths is diﬃcult because their chemical
properties are so similar. Plants are expensive, technically complex
and often tailored to a particular ore body and product mix.
Processing can also generate toxic and, in some cases, radioactive

waste.
Heavy rare earths are the weakest link
The US gap is widest in heavy rare earths. Dysprosium and terbium
are added in small quantities to neodymium-iron-boron magnets
to help them perform at high temperatures. This makes them
particularly important for electric-vehicle motors, aerospace
systems and defence equipment.

### Page 5

US output is concentrated in light rare earths such as neodymium
and praseodymium. Commercial-scale domestic separation of
dysprosium and terbium is only beginning.
MP Materials is developing a heavy rare earth separation line at
Mountain Pass designed to produce around 200 tonnes of
dysprosium and terbium a year. This would be an important
domestic step, but it will not close the gap on its own. Domestic
metallisation and magnet-making capacity also remain limited.

New separation facilities will also need reliable feedstock. Heavy
rare earth production is concentrated in China and Myanmar,
leaving US processors exposed even if domestic separation
expands. Alternative deposits exist, but developing them and
connecting them to metals, alloys and magnet production will take
time.
Magnet dependence is the most immediate
risk
For many strategic applications, the critical import is not an oxide
but a qualiﬁed magnet or a component containing one. High-

performance magnets must meet precise requirements for
strength, heat resistance and durability. New suppliers must be
tested and certiﬁed, making it diﬃcult to switch during a disruption.
US magnet capacity remains small compared with domestic
demand and China’s industrial base. The IEA estimates that, even
after announced projects, diversiﬁed magnet output outside China
would meet well below 20% of demand outside China in 2035. The
project pipeline is strongest in mining and weakest in magnets.

Demand is also rising across electric vehicles, wind turbines,
robotics, data centres and defence. This increases the risk that
diversiﬁed capacity will struggle to keep pace, even if announced
projects proceed.
The defence deadline adds urgency
Rare earths are used in small quantities, but they are diﬃcult to
replace in advanced defence systems. The US Department of

Defence estimates an F-35 requires more than 400kg of rare earth
elements, an Arleigh Burke-class destroyer around 2.4 tonnes and
a Virginia-class submarine around 4.2 tonnes.
The deadline is approaching. From 1 January 2027, US defence
procurement restrictions will extend across the supply chain for
neodymium-iron-boron and samarium-cobalt magnets originating
in China, North Korea, Russia or Iran, subject to limited exceptions.

Rare earth materials are essential to US
defence systems
Figures converted from pounds and rounded
Source: US Department of Defense, ING Research
The US rare earth challenge is industrial rather than geological. Its
vulnerability lies in the diﬃcult stages between the mine and the
ﬁnished component. The second article in this series examines how
Washington is trying to close those gaps through domestic
investment, friend-shoring and recycling.

Source PDF: all report pdf/“AI风险吹哨人”作证：AI巨头“极度无视风险”，AI接管人类文明的可能性约1_3.pdf

Date: 2026-10-06
